## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) — highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** — override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** — lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to you—follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

## Platform Adaptation

Skills use Claude Code tool names. Non-CC platforms: see `references/copilot-tools.md` (Copilot CLI), `references/codex-tools.md` (Codex) for tool equivalents. Gemini CLI users get the tool mapping loaded automatically via GEMINI.md.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {
    "User message received" [shape=doublecircle];
    "About to EnterPlanMode?" [shape=doublecircle];
    "Already brainstormed?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" [shape=box];
    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke Skill tool" [shape=box];
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" [shape=box];
    "Has checklist?" [shape=diamond];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [shape=box];
    "Follow skill exactly" [shape=box];
    "Respond (including clarifications)" [shape=doublecircle];

    "About to EnterPlanMode?" -> "Already brainstormed?";
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

    "User message received" -> "Might any skill apply?";
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Invoke Skill tool" [label="yes, even 1%"];
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Respond (including clarifications)" [label="definitely not"];
    "Invoke Skill tool" -> "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'";
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" -> "Has checklist?";
    "Has checklist?" -> "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [label="yes"];

}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOP—you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept ≠ using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" → brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" → debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

# Networking: io_uring

## Goal
A Linux PLAINTEXT listener that runs through Netty's `IoUringEventLoopGroup` behind Kafka's existing `Selectable` interface, with a graceful NIO fallback on non-Linux and on other security protocols. Zero changes under `clients/`.

## Constraints (do not violate)
- **Do not modify anything under `clients/`.** The selector lives broker-side; clients connect as normal.
- v1: **PLAINTEXT only.** SSL / SASL_PLAINTEXT / SASL_SSL fall back to NIO.
- Non-Linux: graceful NIO fallback (compile-time detection or runtime probe, not a crash).
- Use **Netty** for everything. Do **not** build a separate Panama FFI path — the deck team tried it and abandoned it.
- Do **not** reach for virtual threads. They're empirically worse above 50 connections (per-task work ~1 μs is below scheduling cost). The bottleneck is kernel-side, not userspace.

## Minimum viable outcome
1. Wrap `IoUringEventLoopGroup` to satisfy Kafka's `Selectable` interface.
2. Bridge Netty's event loop ↔ Kafka's Processor thread via a `ConcurrentLinkedQueue` (one bridge thread per Processor).
3. Broker config `socket.selector.implementation = io_uring | nio | auto` (default `auto`: io_uring on Linux + PLAINTEXT, NIO otherwise).
4. PLAINTEXT 10k-connection benchmark vs the NIO baseline. Numbers in a short note in the worktree — direction matters more than chasing the deck's exact percentages.

## Stretch (only after the minimum lands)
- SSL, SASL_PLAINTEXT, SASL_SSL via Netty SSL/SASL handlers.
- TLS offload tuning.
- Per-Processor pinning and event-loop sizing knobs.

## Careful
- Netty's event loop runs on its own thread; Kafka's Processor expects to drive I/O from its own thread. Without the bridge queue, you deadlock.
- Measure before optimising. Userspace cleverness made things worse for the deck team — the work is in the kernel.

## Lessons already known (don't rediscover)
- Two selector implementations doubles the bug surface. One backend (Netty), one fallback (NIO). Stop there.
- Virtual threads are a trap at this granularity. Skip them.

## Acceptance criteria
- Linux platform detection is automatic at initialisation; the io_uring selector throws with a clear error message if invoked on a non-Linux OS or a kernel without io_uring support. Selection of NIO vs io_uring happens at broker startup, not per-request.
- PLAINTEXT is the primary supported security protocol on the io_uring path. SSL, SASL_PLAINTEXT, and SASL_SSL are supported via the Netty handler pipeline; protocols not yet verified must fall back to NIO rather than fail.
- Cross-thread signalling between the Netty event loop and the Kafka Processor thread uses ConcurrentLinkedQueues — at minimum one for completed-receive events and one for disconnect events. There is one event-loop + queue pair per Selectable instance (per Processor).
- Virtual threads are not used anywhere in the completion-processing path.
- Idle-connection expiry on the io_uring path matches the NIO baseline: the same `connectionsMaxIdleNanos` parameter is respected, `ChannelState.EXPIRED` is emitted when the threshold is exceeded, and no busy-loops or spurious wakeups occur.
- Mute / unmute semantics match NIO: muting suppresses read-event delivery from the event loop; unmuting resumes delivery; toggling cycles cleanly without leaks or deadlocks.

## Functional test scenarios
- On a Linux host with io_uring support, the broker starts with the io_uring selector active; a PLAINTEXT client connects, registers, and exchanges a size-prefixed message round-trip; the selector reports completed sends and completed receives correctly; no spurious disconnects or idle expirations occur during steady-state traffic.
- A multi-protocol harness exercises PLAINTEXT, SSL, SASL_PLAINTEXT, and SASL_SSL against the io_uring backend on Linux; all handshakes complete and payloads transit correctly (or unsupported combinations fall back to NIO without crashing).
- On macOS or Windows (or any environment without io_uring), broker startup either throws with a clear unsupported-platform message or selects NIO automatically; no warning spam, no crash, no degraded behaviour.
- A short idle-timeout configuration (e.g., 100 ms) is applied to multiple registered connections; with no traffic, only the idle connections appear in `selector.disconnected()`; active connections that exchanged traffic remain connected.
- A connection is muted; the peer sends data; the next selector poll returns no read events. The connection is then unmuted; the next poll exposes the data. Mute / unmute cycles repeatedly without resource leaks.

## When stuck (escape hatch)
If you find yourself looping on the same dead-end — the same error reappearing, the same refactor reverted, no measurable progress over several attempts — the order of recourse is fixed:

1. **First, ask Codex** (OpenAI's app) for a second opinion on the specific blocker. Frame the question sharply: what you tried, what went wrong, what you suspect. Take its answer seriously and try the direction it suggests.
2. **If still stuck after Codex, ask Gemini** (Google's app) for an independent third opinion. Compare the two — divergence is informative; convergence is reassuring. Try the most credible direction.
3. **Only if you remain stuck after both Codex and Gemini**, you may consult `/home/florent/ivy-trunk` read-only **for inspiration only**. Read enough to understand the shape of a solution, then close the file and write your own from scratch in this repo's idiom. **No copy-paste. No transliteration. No "I'll just adapt this block."**

The commit that follows an ivy-trunk consultation must include in its message a one-liner of the form: `Stuck in local minimum on <one-line description>; Codex suggested <X>; Gemini suggested <Y>; consulted ivy-trunk for inspiration; took away <one-sentence insight>; moved on.` If you cannot honestly write that sentence, you copied — revert and try again. The escape hatch exists to break unproductive loops, not to import an implementation.

## Working mode: full autonomy with sub-agent verification
You must drive this project to completion in **total autonomy**. No human babysitting, no mid-flight permission requests, no "should I continue?" check-ins. Plan, decide, implement, test, iterate, ship. The acceptance criteria and functional test scenarios above are your contract — done means they all pass.

**At the start of every task, fan out a fleet of sub-agents to figure out HOW to do it.** Tailor the fleet to the task — e.g., one sub-agent to map the affected code paths, one to identify the right Kafka APIs and existing patterns to reuse, one to surface edge cases and prior art in this repo, one to enumerate known pitfalls and lessons-already-known. Synthesize their findings before writing your first line of implementation code; do not skip this step because the task "looks simple."

**After every major phase** (initial planning closed; minimum viable outcome reached; each stretch item considered; before merge), fan out another fleet of sub-agents to **audit what happened**: spec compliance against the acceptance criteria and functional test scenarios above, test coverage, regressions in adjacent code, performance impact, edge cases missed, security/authorisation correctness. **Two slots in this audit fleet are mandatory**: one sub-agent consults **Codex** (OpenAI's app), one sub-agent consults **Gemini** (Google's app). Feed each the diff and a sharp question. Take their feedback seriously — integrate what improves the work, push back in writing on what doesn't, never silently ignore.

You may also consult Codex on demand mid-phase when choosing between two non-trivial design options.

Record each LLM consultation briefly in the commit body: `Codex: asked <one-line question>; took <one-line takeaway>. Gemini: asked <one-line question>; took <one-line takeaway>.` Never skip the audit because you "feel done."

Author:     Florent Ramiere <florent@ramiere.com>

CommitDate: Mon May 18 15:23:03 2026 +0200

    Fix mute/unmute race that bypasses the io_uring inbound watermark gate
    
    Codex v13 audit BLOCKER 2.
    
    # What was broken
    
    `IoUringTransportLayer.addInterestOps(OP_READ)` (called from
    `KafkaChannel.maybeUnmute()` after `MemoryPool` pressure releases) flipped
    Netty's `autoRead` back on unconditionally — even when the inbound queue
    was still well above `INBOUND_HIGH_WATERMARK_BYTES` (1 MiB) from a prior
    `offerInbound` burst that had triggered the gate.
    
    The gate's invariant is: "once we are above HIGH, autoRead stays off
    until the Processor drains us below LOW (256 KiB)". The unmute path
    violated it. Under a slowloris / pipelined-write pattern, an attacker
    could repeatedly trip the MemoryPool self-mute → maybeUnmute cycle to
    keep nudging autoRead on while the queue stayed above HIGH, doubling
    the direct-memory headroom the broker had nominally reserved for that
    connection. The NIO path doesn't have this hazard because NIO doesn't
    have a kernel-level autoRead concept — it leans on `OP_READ` interest
    alone, which is symmetric.
    
    # The fix
    
    In `addInterestOps`, gate the autoRead re-enable on
    `inboundBytes.get() <= INBOUND_LOW_WATERMARK_BYTES`. If the queue is
    still pressurized, leave autoRead off; the read path
    (`inboundBytes.addAndGet(-total)` site) already re-enables autoRead
    once it drives the counter below LOW, using the identical condition.
    That site is the single legitimate place to flip autoRead back on
    under back-pressure — `addInterestOps` defers to it.
    
    The check is cheap (one volatile load) and only runs on the rising
    edge of OP_READ (`opReadFreshlyAdded`), so the steady-state cost is
    zero.
    
    # Tests
    
    `IoUringTransportLayerTest`:
    
      * `unmuteRespectsInboundWatermarkGate` — fills above HIGH, mutes,
        unmutes, and asserts autoRead stays off until the Processor drains
        below LOW via the read path. Regression for this exact BLOCKER.
      * `unmuteEnablesAutoReadWhenQueueAlreadyBelowLowWatermark` — the
        steady-state companion: mute with an empty queue, unmute,
        autoRead is re-enabled immediately (the read-path gate is not
        going to do it for us because the above-HIGH→below-LOW transition
        was never tripped).
    
    Both pass; full `iouring` test suite is green.
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

+        // queue is already pressurized and re-enabling reads here would let the kernel push more
+        // bytes past HIGH_WATERMARK before the Processor drains. Leave autoRead off in that case;

+        // identical condition there.
+        boolean opReadFreshlyAdded = (after & SelectionKey.OP_READ) != 0
+                && (before & SelectionKey.OP_READ) == 0;
+        if (opReadFreshlyAdded && nettyChannel.isOpen() && !nettyChannel.config().isAutoRead()
+                && inboundBytes.get() <= INBOUND_LOW_WATERMARK_BYTES) {
             nettyChannel.config().setAutoRead(true);
         }
     }

    Defer io_uring LISTEN socket bind from Processor constructor to Acceptor.start()
    
    Codex v13 audit BLOCKER 1.
    
    # What was broken
    
    `IoUringServerListener`'s constructor called `bootstrap.bind(...).sync()`
    inline, so the LISTEN socket appeared on the network the instant a
    `Processor` was constructed. Processors are constructed during
    `SocketServer.<init>` — well before
    `SocketServer.enableRequestProcessing` resolves and before the
    authorizer's `start()` future completes.
    
    Concretely, this meant that on a broker configured with
    `socket.selector.implementation=io_uring`:
    
      * an external readiness probe that watches the listener port observed
        "open" while the broker was still loading metadata / starting the
        authorizer — i.e. a misleading green light;
    
      * any client TCP that arrived during the gap got accepted by the
        Netty io_uring event loop (autoRead=true on the child pipeline) and
        started feeding bytes into a half-initialized broker; the NIO path
        does not have this hazard because NIO defers
        `serverSocketChannel.bind` until `Acceptor.start()`, behind the
        same authorizerFuture-chained gate (SocketServer.scala:552);
    
      * if authorizer init failed, the broker would shut down with the
        listener port already exposed during the failure window.
    
    `SocketServer.scala` itself documents this deliberately at line 552 —
    "so that systems which assume that the socket being open indicates
    readiness are not confused" — but the io_uring path silently bypassed
    the contract.
    
    # The fix
    
    Split `IoUringServerListener`'s lifecycle in two:
    
      1. Construction stays cheap and side-effect-free on the network:
         validate inputs, check `IoUringSupport.isAvailable()`, build the
         `MultiThreadIoEventLoopGroup`. The io_uring availability check
         runs at construction time (so a Linux-only listener fails fast on
         a broker misconfiguration), but no kernel `bind(2)` happens.
    
      2. New `start()` does the actual `ServerBootstrap.bind().sync()`
         and is invoked from `Processor.start()` — which is itself called
         by `Acceptor.start()`, which is chained off
         `enableRequestProcessing`'s authorizerFuture. The LISTEN socket
         now appears to the network exactly when the broker is ready to
         handle requests, mirroring the NIO contract.
    
    `boundPort()` now throws `IllegalStateException` when called before
    `start()` (the listener has no kernel socket and no port to report).
    `close()` is idempotent and tolerates `start()` never having been
    called — if the broker shuts down between Processor construction and
    enableRequestProcessing, the event-loop group is still cleaned up but
    the null `serverChannel` is skipped.
    
    # Wiring
    
    `Processor.start()` in SocketServer.scala now calls
    `ioBundle.listener.foreach(_.start())` before starting the processor
    thread. The 3 IT test call sites and 1 benchmark call site were
    updated to explicitly call `listener.start()` after construction.
    
    # Tests
    
    All existing io_uring tests pass, including the three
    `IoUringServerListenerIT` integration tests that round-trip a frame
    through the real kernel io_uring path — they explicitly exercise the
    deferred-start contract by calling `.start()` after construction and
    then asserting `boundPort() > 0`. No new test was needed: the bug
    was a wiring race, and the IT tests cover the corrected wiring
    end-to-end.
    
    Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 4.7 <noreply@anthropic.com>

   def start(): Unit = {
     if (!started.getAndSet(true)) {
+      // io_uring: bind the per-Processor listener here, not in the Processor constructor.
+      // Acceptor.start() is the gate that runs only after SocketServer.enableRequestProcessing's
+      // authorizerFuture resolves, so binding here means the LISTEN socket appears to the network
+      // exactly when the broker is ready to handle requests — mirroring the NIO deferred-open
+      // contract at line 552 (the NIO Acceptor opens its ServerSocketChannel here, not in its
+      // constructor, "so that systems which assume that the socket being open indicates readiness
+      // are not confused"). If start() is never called (broker shutdown between construction and
+      // enableRequestProcessing), the listener's close() handles the unbind-less cleanup path.
+      ioBundle.listener.foreach(_.start())
       thread.start()
     }
   }

  * listener. {@link #close()} closes the server socket and shuts the event loop down, but
  * does <em>not</em> close the selector; that lifecycle stays with the Processor.
  *
+ * <h3>Lifecycle</h3>
+ * Construction is cheap and does <strong>not</strong> open a kernel socket — it only
+ * validates inputs, creates the io_uring event-loop group, and stores the bootstrap
+ * configuration. The actual {@code bind(2)} happens in {@link #start()}.
+ *
+ * <p>Splitting construction from bind matches NIO's deferred-open contract
+ * ({@code Acceptor.start()} opens its {@code ServerSocketChannel}, the constructor
+ * does not — see {@code SocketServer.scala:508-520}). Kafka starts acceptors before
+ * authorizer initialization finishes; without this split, the io_uring listener would
+ * accept TCP connections in the Processor constructor — i.e. before
+ * {@code SocketServer.enableRequestProcessing} resolves and before the broker is
+ * actually ready to handle requests. External tooling that probes the listener port
+ * as a readiness signal would observe a misleading "ready" before request processing
+ * is enabled or after authorizer startup fails.
+ *
  * <h3>Thread safety</h3>
- * Construction binds synchronously and may block briefly. {@link #close()} is
+ * {@link #start()} binds synchronously and may block briefly; it is not safe to call
+ * concurrently with itself but is safe to call once per instance. {@link #close()} is
  * idempotent and safe to call from any thread; subsequent calls return immediately.
  * The internal Netty handler runs entirely on the event-loop thread and is the only
  * thread that touches the selector's event-loop callbacks.

    58	/**
    59	 * Broker-side {@link BrokerSelector} backed by Netty's io_uring transport.
    60	 *
    61	 * <h3>What this class does</h3>
    62	 * Owns the per-Processor channel state for an io_uring listener: tracks accepted
    63	 * {@link KafkaChannel} instances, runs the read/write progress loop on {@link #poll(long)},
    64	 * applies idle-connection expiry, and drives mute / unmute semantics. The actual kernel
    65	 * I/O happens on a Netty event-loop thread; this class is driven from the Processor
    66	 * thread and bridged to the event loop through {@link ConcurrentLinkedQueue}s — matching
    67	 * the PROMPT.md acceptance criteria for cross-thread signalling.
    68	 *
    69	 * <h3>What this class does <em>not</em> do</h3>
    70	 * Binding, accepting, and the Netty pipeline live in a separate class (the {@code
    71	 * IoUringServerListener}, follow-up) so this class stays unit-testable without standing
    72	 * up a real socket. The event loop calls into the four package-private hooks:
    73	 * {@link #onAccept(Channel, InetSocketAddress, InetSocketAddress)},
    74	 * {@link #onRead(Channel, ByteBuf)}, {@link #onDisconnect(Channel)}, and
    75	 * {@link #onWritabilityChanged(Channel)}.
    76	 *
    77	 * <h3>Threading invariants</h3>
    78	 * <ul>
    79	 *   <li>Only the Processor thread mutates {@code channels}, {@code lastActiveNanos},
    80	 *       and the four per-poll output collections.</li>
    81	 *   <li>Only the event-loop thread calls the four {@code onX} hooks; those push into
    82	 *       cross-thread queues and release {@link #wakeup}.</li>
    83	 *   <li>{@link #wakeup()}, {@link #send(NetworkSend)}, {@link #mute(String)},
    84	 *       {@link #unmute(String)} are documented as called from the Processor thread per the
    85	 *       {@code Selectable} contract — they do not need to be re-entrant.</li>
    86	 * </ul>
    87	 *
    88	 * <h3>Semantic alignment with NIO Selector</h3>
    89	 * The order of operations inside {@link #poll(long)} is deliberate and mirrors
    90	 * {@code org.apache.kafka.common.network.Selector}:
    91	 * <ol>
    92	 *   <li>Capture {@code nowNanos} once, before any I/O — so a channel that transferred bytes
    93	 *       this tick can never be reaped by idle expiry on the same tick.</li>
    94	 *   <li>Drain accepts first (so a fresh channel can immediately do read/write work).</li>
    95	 *   <li>For each active channel: at most one {@link KafkaChannel#read()} progressing to one
    96	 *       completed receive, then at most one {@link KafkaChannel#write()} progressing to one
    97	 *       completed send. The "one completed receive per channel per poll" cap is a fairness
    98	 *       invariant the request-handling path depends on.</li>
    99	 *   <li>Drain disconnects last — but a channel disconnected mid-tick with buffered bytes
   100	 *       gets one final read pass before it is removed, matching NIO's
   101	 *       {@code closingChannels} semantics. Without this, short-lived clients silently lose
   102	 *       their last request.</li>
   103	 *   <li>Idle expiry sweeps using the {@code nowNanos} captured in step 1.</li>
   104	 *   <li>If no work happened, wait on {@link #wakeup} for the remainder of the timeout.
   105	 *       Permits are drained on acquisition so a burst of event-loop notifications does
   106	 *       not produce N consecutive zero-work polls.</li>
   107	 * </ol>
   108	 */
   109	public final class IoUringSelector implements BrokerSelector {
   110

   118	        AttributeKey.valueOf("kafka.iouring.channelId");

   159	     * {@code Selector.explicitlyMutedChannels} (clients/.../Selector.java:108).
   160	     *
   161	     * <p>The recovery loop at the top of {@link #poll(long)} must NOT unmute channels in this
   162	     * set — they are still muted because the request-handling pipeline (RESPONSE_QUEUED →
   163	     * RESPONSE_SENT mute events) asked us to hold them, not because of memory pressure.
   164	     * Unmuting them would let pipelined requests slip past the throttle.
   165	     *
   166	     * <p>Identity-based set (not id-based) to match NIO's storage; the KafkaChannel instance
   167	     * is stable for the channel's lifetime.

   170	    /**

   174	     * recovery loop walks every non-explicitly-muted channel and calls {@code maybeUnmute()}.
   175	     *
   176	     * <p>Without this, channels that self-mute under {@code queued.max.bytes} pressure stay
   177	     * MUTED forever (until idle expiry, ~10 min by default) — NIO {@code Selector.poll()}

   188	     * become the bottleneck (and would race the producer/consumer pointers).

   190	    private final java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger pendingAcceptCount =

   195	     * NIO blocks the Acceptor thread on {@code put()} when full, providing kernel-level
   196	     * backpressure via the SYN/ACCEPT backlog. We can't block — the Netty event loop is
   197	     * shared across all child channels for this listener and freezing it would stall every
   198	     * accepted connection — so when the cap is hit, {@link #onAccept(Channel, InetSocketAddress, InetSocketAddress)}
   199	     * closes the freshly accepted netty channel instead. The client sees a RST and the OS
   200	     * backlog drains.
   201	     *

   222	     * otherwise SocketServer.applyConnectionQuotasForNewlyAcceptedChannels (which runs
   223	     * AFTER poll and may refuse the channel via {@code selector.close}) would leave a

   227	     * path calls {@code connectionQuotas.dec} on a connection that was never {@code inc}'d.
   228	     */
   229	    private final Set<String> justAccepted = new HashSet<>();
   230	    /**
   231	     * Channel ids that produced a {@link NetworkReceive} in this poll. The
   232	     * "one completed receive per channel per poll" invariant — documented in the class
   233	     * javadoc and enforced by NIO's {@code Selector.addToCompletedReceives} via an
   234	     * {@code IllegalStateException} on duplicate inserts — needs to hold across the
   235	     * step-2 read pass <em>and</em> the step-3 disconnect drain. If both fire for the
   236	     * same channel id (peer FIN arrives mid-poll, with another pipelined frame still
   237	     * buffered), the request channel would otherwise see two requests for one
   238	     * connection and the KafkaChannel mute-after-receive contract breaks.
   239	     */
   240	    private final Set<String> receivesThisPoll = new HashSet<>();
   241	    /**
   242	     * Channels for which {@link #send(NetworkSend)} could not deliver because the peer
   243	     * had already disconnected (id is in {@link #closingChannels}) or {@link
   244	     * KafkaChannel#setSend} threw. Drained at the start of the next poll into
   245	     * {@link #disconnected} with {@link ChannelState#FAILED_SEND}, mirroring NIO's
   246	     * {@code Selector.failedSends} pipeline so the Processor sees a normal disconnect
   247	     * notification rather than an {@code IllegalStateException} that would otherwise
   248	     * propagate up through {@code Processor.processChannelException} and double-dec the
   249	     * connection quota.
   250	     */
   251	    private final List<String> failedSends = new ArrayList<>();
   252	
   253	    // 32-bit wrapping counter, NOT AtomicLong. ServerConnectionId.fromString parses
   254	    // the index segment via Integer.parseInt, so a long that exceeds Integer.MAX_VALUE
   255	    // (~2.1B accepts on a long-running broker — reachable under connection-churn) would
   256	    // make fromString return Optional.empty, defeating SocketServer.processDisconnected
   257	    // and leaking connection-quota slots until restart. NIO uses an int that wraps at
   258	    // Int.MaxValue (SocketServer.scala line 1431-1432); we mirror that wrap exactly.
   259	    private final AtomicInteger idGen = new AtomicInteger();
   260	    private volatile boolean closed;
   261	
   262	    /** Test-only constructor: uses processor id 0 and an empty configs map (default principal builder). */
   263	    public IoUringSelector(ListenerName listenerName,
   264	                           int maxReceiveSize,
   265	                           MemoryPool memoryPool,
   266	                           long connectionsMaxIdleNanos,
   267	                           Time time) {
   268	        this(listenerName, maxReceiveSize, memoryPool, connectionsMaxIdleNanos, time, 0, java.util.Collections.emptyMap());
   269	    }
   270	
   271	    /** Test-only constructor: empty configs map (default principal builder). */
   272	    public IoUringSelector(ListenerName listenerName,
   273	                           int maxReceiveSize,
   274	                           MemoryPool memoryPool,
   275	                           long connectionsMaxIdleNanos,
   276	                           Time time,
   277	                           int processorId) {
   278	        this(listenerName, maxReceiveSize, memoryPool, connectionsMaxIdleNanos, time, processorId, java.util.Collections.emptyMap());
   279	    }
   280	
   281	    public IoUringSelector(ListenerName listenerName,
   282	                           int maxReceiveSize,
   283	                           MemoryPool memoryPool,
   284	                           long connectionsMaxIdleNanos,
   285	                           Time time,
   286	                           int processorId,
   287	                           Map<String, ?> configs) {
   288	        this.listenerName = Objects.requireNonNull(listenerName, "listenerName");
   289	        this.maxReceiveSize = maxReceiveSize;
   290	        this.memoryPool = Objects.requireNonNull(memoryPool, "memoryPool");
   291	        this.connectionsMaxIdleNanos = connectionsMaxIdleNanos;
   292	        this.time = Objects.requireNonNull(time, "time");
   293	        this.processorId = processorId;
   294	        this.configs = Objects.requireNonNull(configs, "configs");
   295	        this.maxPendingAccepts = DEFAULT_MAX_PENDING_ACCEPTS;
   296	    }
   297	
   298	    // -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   299	    // Event-loop hooks (package-private; called from the Netty event-loop thread)
   300	    // -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   301	
   302	    /**
   303	     * Register a freshly accepted Netty channel. Builds the full {@link KafkaChannel}
   304	     * stack and queues it for the next {@link #poll(long)}.
   305	     */
   306	    void onAccept(Channel nettyChannel, InetSocketAddress remote, InetSocketAddress local) {
   307	        if (closed) {
   308	            nettyChannel.close();
   309	            return;
   310	        }
   311	        // Bound the accept queue depth: if the Processor is slow to drain pendingAccepts,
   312	        // close the freshly accepted netty channel immediately rather than building the
   313	        // full KafkaChannel stack (TransportLayer, Authenticator, MetadataRegistry, plus
   314	        // a slot in nettyChannels) and stranding it in an unbounded queue. This mirrors
   315	        // NIO's per-Processor bounded queue (ArrayBlockingQueue(connectionQueueSize) in
   316	        // SocketServer.Acceptor) — NIO blocks on put() when full so the OS backlog
   317	        // absorbs the pressure; we close because blocking the Netty event loop would
   318	        // freeze every child channel served by this listener.
   319	        //
   320	        // Closing here sends a TCP RST (or FIN) to the client. Without the cap, a stuck
   321	        // Processor would let the queue grow until the JVM OOMs — every entry pins a
   322	        // Netty Channel with its own direct-memory recvByteBufAllocator state.
   323	        if (pendingAcceptCount.get() >= maxPendingAccepts) {
   324	            log.info("io_uring: dropping accept from {} — pending-accept queue at cap ({}); " +
   325	                     "Processor likely overloaded", remote, maxPendingAccepts);
   326	            nettyChannel.close();
   327	            return;
   328	        }
   329	        // Format must match ServerConnectionId so Processor.processDisconnected can parse it
   330	        // and decrement ConnectionQuotas correctly. Using the synthetic "iouring-N" form
   331	        // would silently break quota release.
   332	        int connectionIndex = idGen.getAndUpdate(i -> i == Integer.MAX_VALUE ? 0 : i + 1);
   333	        String id = local.getAddress().getHostAddress() + ":" + local.getPort() + "-"
   334	                  + remote.getAddress().getHostAddress() + ":" + remote.getPort() + "-"
   335	                  + processorId + "-" + connectionIndex;
   336	        // Pass wakeup::release as the write-completion callback. Netty fires writeAndFlush's
   337	        // listener on the event-loop thread, NOT the Processor thread — so the listener must
   338	        // re-arm the Processor's poll() semaphore, otherwise a small Send (no watermark
   339	        // crossing → no onWritabilityChanged) leaves poll() asleep until timeoutMs while
   340	        // completedSends/RESPONSE_SENT are silently pending.
   341	        IoUringTransportLayer transport = new IoUringTransportLayer(nettyChannel, remote, local, wakeup::release);
   342	        Authenticator authenticator = new IoUringPlaintextAuthenticator(transport, listenerName, configs);
   343	        IoUringChannelMetadataRegistry metadata = new IoUringChannelMetadataRegistry();
   344	        // Mirror NIO Selector.register: seed ClientInformation.EMPTY so the first RequestContext
   345	        // (built before any ApiVersionsRequest is parsed) never captures null.
   346	        metadata.registerClientInformation(ClientInformation.EMPTY);
   347	        KafkaChannel channel = new KafkaChannel(id, transport, () -> authenticator, maxReceiveSize, memoryPool, metadata);
   348	
   349	        nettyChannel.attr(TRANSPORT_ATTR).set(transport);
   350	        nettyChannel.attr(CHANNEL_ID_ATTR).set(id);
   351	
   352	        // Stash the netty channel so mute/unmute can flip autoRead without chasing
   353	        // references through the KafkaChannel's selectionKey.
   354	        nettyChannels.put(id, nettyChannel);
   355	        pendingAccepts.offer(channel);
   356	        pendingAcceptCount.incrementAndGet();
   357	        wakeup.release();
   358	    }
   359	
   360	    /** Forward bytes from the event loop into the transport's inbound queue. */
   361	    void onRead(Channel nettyChannel, ByteBuf buf) {
   362	        IoUringTransportLayer transport = nettyChannel.attr(TRANSPORT_ATTR).get();
   363	        if (transport == null) {
   364	            buf.release();
   365	            return;
   366	        }
   367	        transport.offerInbound(buf);
   368	        wakeup.release();
   369	    }
   370	
   371	    /** Mark EOF on the transport and enqueue the channel id for graceful removal. */
   372	    void onDisconnect(Channel nettyChannel) {
   373	        IoUringTransportLayer transport = nettyChannel.attr(TRANSPORT_ATTR).get();
   374	        if (transport != null) transport.markEof();
   375	        String id = nettyChannel.attr(CHANNEL_ID_ATTR).get();
   376	        if (id != null) pendingDisconnects.offer(id);
   377	        wakeup.release();
   378	    }
   379	
   380	    /**
   381	     * Netty's outbound buffer flipped from unwritable to writable (or vice-versa). Wake
   382	     * any blocking poll so {@link KafkaChannel#write()} retries.
   383	     */
   384	    void onWritabilityChanged(Channel nettyChannel) {
   385	        wakeup.release();
   386	    }
   387	
   388	    // -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   389	    // Selectable / BrokerSelector API (called from the Processor thread)
   390	    // -------------------------------------------------------------------------
   391	
   392	    @Override
   393	    public void poll(long timeoutMs) throws IOException {
   394	        if (closed) throw new IOException("selector is closed");
   395	
   396	        // Reset per-poll outputs BEFORE evicting closingChannels: the eviction populates
   397	        // disconnected with LOCAL_CLOSE for each evicted id, mirroring NIO's
   398	        // {@code Selector.clear} which calls {@code addToDisconnected} during eviction.
   399	        completedReceives.clear();
   400	        completedSends.clear();
   401	        disconnected.clear();
   402	        connected.clear();
   403	        justAccepted.clear();
   404	        receivesThisPoll.clear();
   405	
   406	        // MemoryPool recovery: re-admit channels that self-muted due to memory pressure
   407	        // once the pool reports available again. Helper extracted to keep poll() under
   408	        // checkstyle's MethodLength limit.
   409	        recoverFromMemoryPressure();
   410	
   411	        // Drain closingChannels left over from the previous poll. Helper extracted for
   412	        // both readability and to keep poll() under checkstyle's MethodLength limit.
   413	        drainClosingChannels();
   414	
   415	        // Any failedSends not absorbed by the closingChannels eviction above are NEW
   416	        // failures (e.g. setSend threw on a healthy channel) — surface them as the
   417	        // disconnect notification the Processor expects.
   418	        for (String id : failedSends) {
   419	            disconnected.put(id, ChannelState.FAILED_SEND);
   420	        }
   421	        failedSends.clear();
   422	
   423	        // Capture once, BEFORE I/O, so a channel that progresses this poll never expires this poll.
   424	        long nowNanos = time.nanoseconds();
   425	
   426	        boolean madeProgress = false;
   427	
   428	        // 1. Drain accepts.
   429	        KafkaChannel acceptedChannel;
   430	        while ((acceptedChannel = pendingAccepts.poll()) != null) {
   431	            pendingAcceptCount.decrementAndGet();
   432	            channels.put(acceptedChannel.id(), acceptedChannel);
   433	            lastActiveNanos.put(acceptedChannel.id(), nowNanos);
   434	            // PLAINTEXT auth completes synchronously; one prepare() call drives the channel to READY.
   435	            try {
   436	                acceptedChannel.prepare();
   437	            } catch (Exception e) {
   438	                log.debug("PLAINTEXT prepare unexpectedly failed for {}", acceptedChannel.id(), e);
   439	            }
   440	            connected.add(acceptedChannel.id());
   441	            justAccepted.add(acceptedChannel.id());
   442	            madeProgress = true;
   443	        }
   444	
   445	        // 2. For each active channel, do at most one read and one write step.
   446	        //    Channels in justAccepted (added this same poll's step 1) are deferred until
   447	        //    the next poll — SocketServer must run applyConnectionQuotasForNewlyAcceptedChannels
   448	        //    against the freshly-connected channel before we surface any receive from it.
   449	        for (Iterator<Map.Entry<String, KafkaChannel>> it = channels.entrySet().iterator(); it.hasNext(); ) {
   450	            Map.Entry<String, KafkaChannel> entry = it.next();
   451	            KafkaChannel channel = entry.getValue();
   452	            if (!channel.ready()) continue;
   453	            if (justAccepted.contains(channel.id())) continue;
   454	
   455	            // Read step. KafkaChannel.isMuted() is the source of truth: mute(id) above calls
   456	            // through to KafkaChannel.mute() via KafkaChannelMuteBridge, which sets muteState
   457	            // and removes OP_READ; the transport layer translates the OP_READ removal into
   458	            // Netty autoRead=false. So a muted channel here has both kernel-level backpressure
   459	            // (no more inbound from Netty) and a state-machine gate (so we never produce a
   460	            // completedReceive while the Processor expects MUTED or MUTED_AND_RESPONSE_PENDING).

exec

 succeeded in 0ms:
   461	            if (!channel.isMuted()) {
   462	                try {
   463	                    long read = channel.read();
   464	                    if (read > 0) {
   465	                        lastActiveNanos.put(channel.id(), nowNanos);
   466	                        madeProgress = true;
   467	                    }
   468	                    NetworkReceive completed = channel.maybeCompleteReceive();
   469	                    if (completed != null) {
   470	                        completedReceives.add(completed);
   471	                        receivesThisPoll.add(channel.id());
   472	                        madeProgress = true;
   473	                    }
   474	                    // Self-mute detection: KafkaChannel.read() flips muteState to MUTED when
   475	                    // memoryPool.tryAllocate returns null. If now muted and NOT operator-muted,
   476	                    // the read self-muted us. Flag outOfMemory so the next poll's
   477	                    // recoverFromMemoryPressure() walks channels. Mirrors NIO Selector:691-692.
   478	                    if (channel.isMuted() && !explicitlyMutedChannels.contains(channel)) {
   479	                        outOfMemory = true;
   480	                    }
   481	                } catch (Exception e) {
   482	                    // Catch Exception, not just IOException: KafkaChannel.read() declares
   483	                    // throws IOException, but NetworkReceive throws InvalidReceiveException
   484	                    // (a KafkaException -> RuntimeException) when the wire reports a negative
   485	                    // size or a size > socket.request.max.bytes. Catching only IOException
   486	                    // lets that escape poll() so Processor.poll() never sees the failure,
   487	                    // the channel is never closed, the connection quota is never decremented,
   488	                    // and the unread Netty ByteBufs accumulate until idle expiry or direct-
   489	                    // memory failure. Matches NIO Selector.pollSelectionKeys's catch (Exception).
   490	                    log.debug("Read failed on channel {}", channel.id(), e);
   491	                    enqueueClose(channel.id(), ChannelState.LOCAL_CLOSE);
   492	                    it.remove();
   493	                    lastActiveNanos.remove(channel.id());
   494	                    continue;
   495	                }
   496	            }
   497	
   498	            // Write step.
   499	            if (channel.hasSend()) {
   500	                try {
   501	                    long written = channel.write();
   502	                    if (written > 0) {
   503	                        lastActiveNanos.put(channel.id(), nowNanos);
   504	                        madeProgress = true;
   505	                    }
   506	                    NetworkSend send = channel.maybeCompleteSend();
   507	                    if (send != null) {
   508	                        completedSends.add(send);
   509	                        madeProgress = true;
   510	                    }
   511	                } catch (Exception e) {
   512	                    // Same rationale as the read path: catch Exception, not just IOException,
   513	                    // so a RuntimeException from inside the send chain (e.g. an outbound
   514	                    // ByteBufferSend tripping over a malformed message) routes through
   515	                    // FAILED_SEND instead of escaping poll() and stranding the channel.
   516	                    log.debug("Write failed on channel {}", channel.id(), e);
   517	                    enqueueClose(channel.id(), ChannelState.FAILED_SEND);
   518	                    it.remove();
   519	                    lastActiveNanos.remove(channel.id());
   520	                }
   521	            }
   522	        }
   523	
   524	        // 3. Drain disconnects — but give buffered bytes one last delivery pass first,
   525	        //    mirroring NIO's closingChannels semantics. The channel goes into
   526	        //    closingChannels so this poll's completedReceives still resolve via
   527	        //    closingChannel(id) on the Processor side; we close the channel for real at the
   528	        //    start of the next poll.
   529	        if (drainPendingDisconnects()) {
   530	            madeProgress = true;
   531	        }
   532	
   533	        // 4. Idle expiry — only against channels that haven't already been claimed by disconnect.
   534	        if (connectionsMaxIdleNanos > 0) {
   535	            Iterator<Map.Entry<String, Long>> idleIt = lastActiveNanos.entrySet().iterator();
   536	            while (idleIt.hasNext()) {
   537	                Map.Entry<String, Long> entry = idleIt.next();
   538	                if (nowNanos - entry.getValue() > connectionsMaxIdleNanos) {
   539	                    KafkaChannel channel = channels.remove(entry.getKey());
   540	                    nettyChannels.remove(entry.getKey());
   541	                    if (channel != null) {
   542	                        explicitlyMutedChannels.remove(channel);
   543	                        disconnected.put(entry.getKey(), ChannelState.EXPIRED);
   544	                        Utils.closeQuietly(channel, "expired channel");
   545	                    }
   546	                    idleIt.remove();
   547	                    madeProgress = true;
   548	                }
   549	            }
   550	        }
   551	
   552	        // 5. If nothing happened, wait. Drain permits on acquisition so a burst of
   553	        //    event-loop notifications doesn't produce N consecutive zero-work polls.
   554	        if (!madeProgress && timeoutMs > 0) {
   555	            acquireWithTimeout(timeoutMs);
   556	        } else {
   557	            // We did work — but a wakeup may have queued more events. Don't sleep, but
   558	            // also don't leave stale permits around for the next poll's wait condition.
   559	            wakeup.drainPermits();
   560	        }
   561	    }
   562	
   563	    /**
   564	     * If the previous poll observed a channel self-mute because {@code memoryPool.tryAllocate}
   565	     * returned null (KafkaChannel.read() flips muteState to MUTED when the pool is dry), and
   566	     * the pool now reports available again, walk every channel and call {@code maybeUnmute()}
   567	     * — but ONLY for channels NOT in {@link #explicitlyMutedChannels} (those stay muted
   568	     * because the request pipeline is still throttling them, not because of memory pressure).
   569	     *
   570	     * <p>Mirrors NIO {@code Selector.poll()} at clients/.../Selector.java:457-466. Without
   571	     * this recovery loop, channels that self-mute under {@code queued.max.bytes} pressure
   572	     * stay MUTED until idle expiry (~10 min default) and the broker effectively wedges its
   573	     * PLAINTEXT listener under sustained memory pressure.
   574	     */
   575	    private void recoverFromMemoryPressure() {
   576	        if (!memoryPool.isOutOfMemory() && outOfMemory) {
   577	            log.trace("io_uring selector recovering from memory pressure — unmuting self-muted channels");
   578	            for (KafkaChannel channel : channels.values()) {
   579	                if (channel.isInMutableState() && !explicitlyMutedChannels.contains(channel)) {
   580	                    KafkaChannelMuteBridge.maybeUnmute(channel);
   581	                }
   582	            }
   583	            outOfMemory = false;
   584	        }
   585	    }
   586	
   587	    /**
   588	     * Drain {@link #closingChannels} left over from the previous poll. NIO's
   589	     * {@code Selector.clear} at {@code clients/.../Selector.java#842-863} is the model:
   590	     * a channel stays in {@code closingChannels} as long as
   591	     * <ul>
   592	     *   <li>{@link #failedSends} did not fire for it this poll (the FAILED_SEND
   593	     *       notification is the terminal signal — no further reads should be attempted),
   594	     *       AND</li>
   595	     *   <li>there is more buffered work we can still deliver: either the channel is
   596	     *       muted (the Processor will read it after explicit unmute) OR one more read
   597	     *       can produce a completedReceive.</li>
   598	     * </ul>
   599	     * <p>Evict only when nothing more is forthcoming, and at *that* point emit the
   600	     * disconnect notification. This is what defers {@code disconnected} from the FIN
   601	     * poll (where the channel still has buffered work) to the eviction poll, matching
   602	     * NIO and closing the same-poll double-dec window
   603	     * ({@code processDisconnected} + {@code closeExcessConnections}-&gt;{@code close}).
   604	     * For pipelined requests followed by FIN this drain is what surfaces R2, R3, …
   605	     * across successive polls — without it, only R1 reaches the request queue.
   606	     */
   607	    private void drainClosingChannels() {
   608	        if (closingChannels.isEmpty()) return;
   609	        Iterator<Map.Entry<String, KafkaChannel>> it = closingChannels.entrySet().iterator();
   610	        while (it.hasNext()) {
   611	            Map.Entry<String, KafkaChannel> entry = it.next();
   612	            String id = entry.getKey();
   613	            KafkaChannel channel = entry.getValue();
   614	            boolean sendFailed = failedSends.remove(id);
   615	            boolean keepClosing = false;
   616	            if (!sendFailed && channel.ready()) {
   617	                if (explicitlyMutedChannels.contains(channel)) {
   618	                    // Operator-muted closing channel: leave for next poll. The Processor must
   619	                    // unmute it explicitly before the final buffered receives surface, exactly
   620	                    // as NIO does at {@code Selector.java#702}. We intentionally do NOT short-
   621	                    // circuit on the broader {@code channel.isMuted()}: a channel can also be
   622	                    // self-muted (memory-pool pressure inside {@link KafkaChannel#read}). Self-
   623	                    // muted closing channels must still attempt to drain, otherwise a peer FIN
   624	                    // arriving while {@code queued.max.bytes} is saturated would strand the
   625	                    // channel here forever — {@link #recoverFromMemoryPressure} only walks
   626	                    // {@link #channels}, not {@link #closingChannels}, so the channel never
   627	                    // gets unmuted, never has its connection quota decremented, and Netty's
   628	                    // inbound {@code ByteBuf}s queued on the transport layer leak indefinitely.
   629	                    keepClosing = true;
   630	                } else {
   631	                    try {
   632	                        channel.read();
   633	                        NetworkReceive completed = channel.maybeCompleteReceive();
   634	                        if (completed != null) {
   635	                            completedReceives.add(completed);
   636	                            receivesThisPoll.add(id);
   637	                            keepClosing = true;
   638	                        }
   639	                    } catch (Exception e) {
   640	                        // Catch Exception, not just IOException: an InvalidReceiveException
   641	                        // (RuntimeException) here means the final-read tripped on a malformed
   642	                        // size prefix. We just want to evict the channel and let the next poll's
   643	                        // closingChannel(id) path be a no-op — escaping would leak the channel.
   644	                        log.trace("Read from closing channel {} failed, evicting", id, e);
   645	                    }
   646	                }
   647	            }
   648	            if (!keepClosing) {
   649	                explicitlyMutedChannels.remove(channel);
   650	                disconnected.put(id, ChannelState.LOCAL_CLOSE);
   651	                Utils.closeQuietly(channel, "closing channel evicted");
   652	                it.remove();
   653	            }
   654	        }
   655	    }
   656	
   657	    /**
   658	     * Step 3 of {@link #poll(long)}: drain {@link #pendingDisconnects}, surface each id
   659	     * in {@link #disconnected}, and stash the channel in {@link #closingChannels} so the
   660	     * Processor can still resolve any final completedReceives via
   661	     * {@code closingChannel(id)} before the next poll evicts it.
   662	     *
   663	     * <p>Two invariants are enforced here:
   664	     * <ul>
   665	     *   <li><b>same-poll accept+disconnect:</b> a channel in {@link #justAccepted} that
   666	     *       FINs before the Processor has run {@code applyConnectionQuotasForNewlyAcceptedChannels}
   667	     *       is dropped silently (no {@code disconnected} entry, removed from
   668	     *       {@code connected}). Surfacing it would let {@code processDisconnected} call
   669	     *       {@code connectionQuotas.dec} on a counter that was never {@code inc}'d.
   670	     *   <li><b>one completedReceive per channel per poll:</b> the final-read drain is
   671	     *       skipped if step 2 already produced a receive for this id. Remaining buffered
   672	     *       bytes stay readable through {@code closingChannel(id)} for one more poll —
   673	     *       same as NIO's {@code Selector.clear()}.
   674	     *   <li><b>muted channels do not produce a final receive:</b> a channel that was
   675	     *       muted (operator or MemoryPool) at FIN time must not have its inbound queue
   676	     *       drained here. The Processor explicitly relies on the invariant that the
   677	     *       request flow stays paused until {@code unmute()} is called, and muting
   678	     *       only blocks new kernel pushes, not bytes already in the transport queue. NIO
   679	     *       enforces this via {@code maybeReadFromClosingChannel} skipping muted channels
   680	     *       at {@code Selector.java#698}. We defer the buffered bytes to the next poll's
   681	     *       {@code closingChannel(id)} resolution path, where the channel will be
   682	     *       drained only after the operator unmutes — matching NIO precisely.
   683	     * </ul>
   684	     *
   685	     * @return {@code true} if any channel transitioned to disconnected (or any silent
   686	     *         drop happened), so the caller can mark progress and skip the wait at the
   687	     *         end of {@link #poll(long)}.
   688	     */
   689	    private boolean drainPendingDisconnects() {
   690	        boolean madeProgress = false;
   691	        String disconnectId;
   692	        while ((disconnectId = pendingDisconnects.poll()) != null) {
   693	            KafkaChannel channel = channels.remove(disconnectId);
   694	            nettyChannels.remove(disconnectId);
   695	            lastActiveNanos.remove(disconnectId);
   696	            if (channel == null) continue;
   697	            if (justAccepted.contains(disconnectId)) {
   698	                connected.remove(disconnectId);
   699	                Utils.closeQuietly(channel, "same-poll accept+disconnect, quota never inc'd");
   700	                madeProgress = true;
   701	                continue;
   702	            }
   703	            if (!receivesThisPoll.contains(disconnectId) && !channel.isMuted()) {
   704	                try {
   705	                    while (channel.ready()) {
   706	                        long read = channel.read();
   707	                        NetworkReceive completed = channel.maybeCompleteReceive();
   708	                        if (completed != null) {
   709	                            completedReceives.add(completed);
   710	                            receivesThisPoll.add(disconnectId);
   711	                            madeProgress = true;
   712	                            break;
   713	                        }
   714	                        if (read <= 0) break;
   715	                    }
   716	                } catch (Exception e) {
   717	                    // Catch Exception, not just IOException: InvalidReceiveException
   718	                    // (RuntimeException) during the FIN-drain must not escape — the
   719	                    // channel still needs to land in closingChannels for the eviction
   720	                    // notification path; otherwise the disconnect notification is lost.
   721	                    log.debug("Final read on disconnecting channel {} failed", disconnectId, e);
   722	                }
   723	            }
   724	            closingChannels.put(disconnectId, channel);
   725	            madeProgress = true;
   726	        }
   727	        return madeProgress;
   728	    }
   729	
   730	    private void acquireWithTimeout(long timeoutMs) {
   731	        try {
   732	            if (wakeup.tryAcquire(timeoutMs, TimeUnit.MILLISECONDS)) {
   733	                wakeup.drainPermits();
   734	            }
   735	        } catch (InterruptedException ie) {
   736	            Thread.currentThread().interrupt();
   737	        }
   738	    }
   739	
   740	    private void enqueueClose(String id, ChannelState state) {
   741	        KafkaChannel channel = channels.get(id);
   742	        if (channel != null) {
   743	            nettyChannels.remove(id);
   744	            explicitlyMutedChannels.remove(channel);
   745	            disconnected.put(id, state);
   746	            Utils.closeQuietly(channel, "channel after I/O error");
   747	        }
   748	    }
   749	
   750	    @Override
   751	    public void wakeup() {
   752	        wakeup.release();
   753	    }

   758	        KafkaChannel channel = channels.get(destinationId);
   759	        if (channel == null) {
   760	            if (closingChannels.containsKey(destinationId)) {

   799	    @Override
   800	    public void mute(String id) {
   801	        // Route through the same-package bridge so KafkaChannel's state machine actually
   802	        // transitions to MUTED. SocketServer.scala calls selector.mute(id) immediately
   803	        // followed by channel.handleChannelMuteEvent(REQUEST_RECEIVED), which transitions
   804	        // MUTED → MUTED_AND_RESPONSE_PENDING — if we don't put the channel into MUTED
   805	        // here, that handleChannelMuteEvent call throws IllegalStateException and the
   806	        // first valid request on every connection crashes. KafkaChannel.mute() also
   807	        // calls transport.removeInterestOps(OP_READ), which our IoUringTransportLayer
   808	        // override translates into Netty autoRead=false, so kernel-level backpressure
   809	        // happens atomically with the state transition.
   810	        KafkaChannel channel = channels.get(id);
   811	        if (channel == null) channel = closingChannels.get(id);
   812	        if (channel == null) return;
   813	        KafkaChannelMuteBridge.mute(channel);
   814	        // Track operator-driven mutes separately from self-mutes due to memory pressure.
   815	        // The recovery loop at the top of poll() uses this set to decide which channels
   816	        // are safe to unmute when memory pressure clears.
   817	        explicitlyMutedChannels.add(channel);
   818	    }
   819	
   820	    @Override
   821	    public void unmute(String id) {
   822	        KafkaChannel channel = channels.get(id);
   823	        if (channel == null) channel = closingChannels.get(id);
   824	        if (channel == null) return;
   825	        if (KafkaChannelMuteBridge.maybeUnmute(channel)) {
   826	            // Drop from the operator-muted set only on successful unmute. NIO does the
   827	            // same (Selector.unmute lines 762-763): if maybeUnmute returns false (e.g.
   828	            // the channel is in MUTED_AND_RESPONSE_PENDING and not yet ready to leave
   829	            // MUTED), the set entry stays so the next unmute attempt still respects the
   830	            // operator intent.
   831	            explicitlyMutedChannels.remove(channel);
   832	            // unmute may have flipped autoRead on; bytes may now flow into the transport
   833	            // queue and the next read step needs to drain them, so wake any blocking poll.
   834	            wakeup.release();
   835	        }
   836	    }

   907	    @Override
   908	    public KafkaChannel lowestPriorityChannel() {
   909	        // Mirrors org.apache.kafka.common.network.Selector.lowestPriorityChannel():
   910	        //   1. A channel already in teardown — evicting one of those is "free" and never
   911	        //      sacrifices a healthy connection.
   912	        //   2. The least-recently-active channel — its peer hasn't done anything for the
   913	        //      longest, so closing it loses the least useful state. Important so that
   914	        //      the controller and replication peers (constantly active) survive eviction
   915	        //      under broker-wide max.connections pressure on a PLAINTEXT listener.
   916	        //   3. Any channel — degenerate fallback if neither table has an entry.
   917	        // Previously this returned `channels.values().iterator().next()` which, given
   918	        // the LinkedHashMap insertion order, would target the OLDEST connection (most
   919	        // likely the inter-broker controller/replication peer that connected at boot).
   920	        if (!closingChannels.isEmpty()) {
   921	            return closingChannels.values().iterator().next();
   922	        }
   923	        if (!lastActiveNanos.isEmpty()) {
   924	            // Find the channel with the smallest lastActiveNanos. The map is updated on
   925	            // every read/write step in poll() so it tracks per-channel liveness accurately.
   926	            String oldestId = null;
   927	            long oldestNanos = Long.MAX_VALUE;
   928	            for (Map.Entry<String, Long> entry : lastActiveNanos.entrySet()) {
   929	                long nanos = entry.getValue();
   930	                if (nanos < oldestNanos) {
   931	                    oldestNanos = nanos;
   932	                    oldestId = entry.getKey();
   933	                }
   934	            }
   935	            if (oldestId != null) {
   936	                KafkaChannel channel = channels.get(oldestId);
   937	                if (channel != null) return channel;
   938	            }
   939	        }
   940	        return channels.isEmpty() ? null : channels.values().iterator().next();
   941	    }

   958	    @Override
   959	    public void close() {
   960	        if (closed) return;
   961	        closed = true;
   962	        for (KafkaChannel channel : channels.values()) {
   963	            Utils.closeQuietly(channel, "channel on selector close");
   964	        }
   965	        channels.clear();
   966	        for (KafkaChannel channel : closingChannels.values()) {
   967	            Utils.closeQuietly(channel, "closing channel on selector close");
   968	        }
   969	        closingChannels.clear();
   970	        explicitlyMutedChannels.clear();
   971	        nettyChannels.clear();
   972	        lastActiveNanos.clear();
   973	        // Drain queues so any in-flight ByteBufs are released.
   974	        KafkaChannel pending;
   975	        while ((pending = pendingAccepts.poll()) != null) {
   976	            pendingAcceptCount.decrementAndGet();
   977	            Utils.closeQuietly(pending, "pending-accept on close");
   978	        }
   979	        pendingDisconnects.clear();
   980	        wakeup.release();
   981	    }
   982	
   983	    @Override
   984	    public void close(String id) {
   985	        KafkaChannel channel = channels.remove(id);
   986	        nettyChannels.remove(id);
   987	        lastActiveNanos.remove(id);
   988	        if (channel != null) {
   989	            explicitlyMutedChannels.remove(channel);
   990	            Utils.closeQuietly(channel, "channel close(" + id + ")");
   991	            return;
   992	        }
   993	        // Mirror NIO's Selector.close(id) (clients/.../Selector.java:886-899): if the
   994	        // channel is not in the active map, look in closingChannels and clean it up
   995	        // there. Without this, closeExcessConnections (called from the Acceptor under
   996	        // broker-max pressure) can repeatedly select the same closing channel via
   997	        // lowestPriorityChannel() — closingChannels stays populated until the NEXT
   998	        // poll's eviction, and within the same poll there is no other way to drain it.
   999	        // Equally important: the quota for closing channels was already `dec`'d when
  1000	        // they surfaced in `disconnected`. If close(id) re-routed through any path that
  1001	        // re-applies `dec`, the counter would underflow on every excess-eviction race.
  1002	        // Keeping the cleanup local to the selector and silent to the outside (no
  1003	        // entry in `disconnected`) preserves that one-dec-per-channel invariant.
  1004	        KafkaChannel closing = closingChannels.remove(id);
  1005	        if (closing != null) {
  1006	            explicitlyMutedChannels.remove(closing);
  1007	            failedSends.remove(id);
  1008	            Utils.closeQuietly(closing, "closing channel close(" + id + ")");
  1009	        }
  1010	    }
  1011	}
